## What I assumed you need

- **R1 (must):** consumption per household, not feeder or grid totals.
- **R2 (must):** at least a year of history per household.
- **R3 (must):** hourly or finer resolution.
- **R4 (nice to have):** downloadable now, without an application.
- **Assumed:** non-commercial research use, and that a few thousand households counts as "hundreds or more".

## Shortlist

Short answer: you can download all of these now. None needs an application. Checked 2026-11-12.

| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Households | Resolution / span | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | GoiEner (Spain) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 25,559 supply points | Hourly, about 3 years on average (2014–2022) | Registry record and page, today |
| 2 | Low Carbon London (UK Power Networks) | Open download, London Datastore | CC BY | 5,567 | 30 min, Nov 2011–Feb 2014 | Web search summary; the page itself is not machine-confirmed |
| 3 | ECD-UY (Uruguay) | Open download, figshare | CC0 | 110,953 | 15 min, about 1.8 years | Registry record; the page blocked scripts, so open it in a browser |
| 4 | Swiss residential load profiles | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 2,447 | 15 min, 2023–2024 | Registry record and page, today |
| 5 | HEAPO (Zurich) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 1,408 | 15 min, Nov 2018–Mar 2024 | Registry record and page, today |
| 6 | iFlex (Norway) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | About 3,700–4,500 (sources disagree) | Hourly, early 2020–spring 2021 | Registry record and page, today |
| 7 | Smart-Grid Smart-City (Australia) | Open download, data.gov.au | CC BY 3.0 AU | Unclear (about 8,000 to 78,000) | 30 min, 2010–2014 | Web search summary only |

## Details

- **GoiEner:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/7362094) has raw data (15.1 GB) and imputed files for series longer than a year. It is a mix of customers. The `contracted_tariff` field lets you keep households, but the "2.X" tariff also includes small businesses. Zenodo also lists newer versions than the v1 I read. The dataset paper is [Sci. Data 2023](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41597-023-02846-0).
- **London:** the [London Datastore page](https://data.london.gov.uk/dataset/smartmeter-energy-consumption-data-in-london-households-vqm0d) offers a zip of about 765 MB. Households join and leave at different times, so filter for those with a full year. This is the most-used household benchmark, and one day-ahead study on it is [Benchmarking of Load Forecasting Methods Using Residential Smart Meter Data](https://doi.org/10.3390/app12199844). Use this official copy rather than the Kaggle or GitHub mirrors.
- **ECD-UY:** by far the largest. The [figshare record](https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.16965400) is CC0, and its paper is [Sci. Data 2022](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41597-022-01122-x). It is a southern-hemisphere climate with little weather metadata.
- **Swiss:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/21398769) was published in July 2026. It covers apartments and houses, including homes with heat pumps, EVs and PV. It has two full years but few published baselines yet.
- **HEAPO:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/15056919) has the longest history, but every home has a heat pump, so demand is strongly weather-driven and unrepresentative.
- **iFlex:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/8248802) includes surveys. It comes from a price-response experiment during COVID, which distorts day-ahead patterns, and it spans only about a year. Check Table 1 of its paper for exact counts.
- **Smart-Grid Smart-City:** the [data.gov.au page](https://data.gov.au/data/dataset/smart-grid-smart-city-customer-trial-data) has half-hourly readings per household. The trial's tariffs and in-home displays may affect behaviour.

For your benchmark, I'd start with GoiEner, London and ECD-UY. They cover three different countries and climates.

## Known, not confirmed

- **Irish CER Smart Metering Trial (ISSDA, UCD):** about 5,000 homes and businesses at 30 min over 2009–2010, about 1.5 years. It is widely used in the literature.
  - **Access:** the search results (not the page) say you complete and sign a request form and email it to ISSDA. That is an application rather than a download. Time to access is unknown, probably days to weeks.
  - **What I tried:** the ISSDA site refused automated requests (HTTP 403), so I couldn't read the terms.
  - **What to check:** the [ISSDA dataset page](https://www.ucd.ie/issda/data/commissionforenergyregulationcer/) for the form and terms.

## Not recommended, and why

- **Pecan Street Dataport:** the free academic tier covers only 75 homes. More needs a paid licence (from web search).
- **Ausgrid Solar Home data:** 300 solar homes at 30 min over 3 years. It is small and PV-skewed, and a repo note says it may have been removed from Ausgrid's site. Public copies exist on [data.gov.au](https://www.data.gov.au/data/en/dataset/nsw-solar-home-electricty-data).
- **IDEAL Household Energy Dataset:** I recall roughly 255 homes, from memory and not confirmed. That is small for your threshold.
- **Kaggle, Hugging Face and Zenodo re-uploads of the London data:** these are third-party copies. Use the official source.

## What I could not find

- I did not check the documentation of every dataset for the share of households with a complete year. Do this yourself for London, iFlex and SGSC.
- Registry searches on DataCite and Hugging Face sometimes returned malformed responses, so the list may be incomplete. I searched Zenodo, OpenAIRE, DataCite, Hugging Face and PhysioNet, plus the web.
- The London and SGSC access and licence details come from a web search summary, not the registry record.

## Next step

Download GoiEner and London first and build your day-ahead baseline on them. Then validate on ECD-UY. If you want an Irish comparison, send the ISSDA request now, since it's the only one with a wait.

The interactive page is at `/private/tmp/e-qVHPie/home/cwd/dataset-shortlist.html`. I didn't commit anything.

## Shortlist

Short answer: you can download all of these now. None needs an application. Checked 2026-11-12.

| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Households | Resolution / span | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | GoiEner (Spain) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 25,559 supply points | Hourly, about 3 years on average (2014–2022) | Registry record and page, today |
| 2 | Low Carbon London (UK Power Networks) | Open download, London Datastore | CC BY | 5,567 | 30 min, Nov 2011–Feb 2014 | Web search summary; the page itself is not machine-confirmed |
| 3 | ECD-UY (Uruguay) | Open download, figshare | CC0 | 110,953 | 15 min, about 1.8 years | Registry record; the page blocked scripts, so open it in a browser |
| 4 | Swiss residential load profiles | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 2,447 | 15 min, 2023–2024 | Registry record and page, today |
| 5 | HEAPO (Zurich) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 1,408 | 15 min, Nov 2018–Mar 2024 | Registry record and page, today |
| 6 | iFlex (Norway) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | About 3,700–4,500 (sources disagree) | Hourly, early 2020–spring 2021 | Registry record and page, today |
| 7 | Smart-Grid Smart-City (Australia) | Open download, data.gov.au | CC BY 3.0 AU | Unclear (about 8,000 to 78,000) | 30 min, 2010–2014 | Web search summary only |

## Details

- **GoiEner:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/7362094) has raw data (15.1 GB) and imputed files for series longer than a year. It is a mix of customers. The `contracted_tariff` field lets you keep households, but the "2.X" tariff also includes small businesses. Zenodo also lists newer versions than the v1 I read. The dataset paper is [Sci. Data 2023](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41597-023-02846-0).
- **London:** the [London Datastore page](https://data.london.gov.uk/dataset/smartmeter-energy-consumption-data-in-london-households-vqm0d) offers a zip of about 765 MB. Households join and leave at different times, so filter for those with a full year. This is the most-used household benchmark, and one day-ahead study on it is [Benchmarking of Load Forecasting Methods Using Residential Smart Meter Data](https://doi.org/10.3390/app12199844). Use this official copy rather than the Kaggle or GitHub mirrors.
- **ECD-UY:** by far the largest. The [figshare record](https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.16965400) is CC0, and its paper is [Sci. Data 2022](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41597-022-01122-x). It is a southern-hemisphere climate with little weather metadata.
- **Swiss:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/21398769) was published in July 2026. It covers apartments and houses, including homes with heat pumps, EVs and PV. It has two full years but few published baselines yet.
- **HEAPO:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/15056919) has the longest history, but every home has a heat pump, so demand is strongly weather-driven and unrepresentative.
- **iFlex:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/8248802) includes surveys. It comes from a price-response experiment during COVID, which distorts day-ahead patterns, and it spans only about a year. Check Table 1 of its paper for exact counts.
- **Smart-Grid Smart-City:** the [data.gov.au page](https://data.gov.au/data/dataset/smart-grid-smart-city-customer-trial-data) has half-hourly readings per household. The trial's tariffs and in-home displays may affect behaviour.

For your benchmark, I'd start with GoiEner, London and ECD-UY. They cover three different countries and climates.

## Known, not confirmed

- **Irish CER Smart Metering Trial (ISSDA, UCD):** about 5,000 homes and businesses at 30 min over 2009–2010, about 1.5 years. It is widely used in the literature.
  - **Access:** the search results (not the page) say you complete and sign a request form and email it to ISSDA. That is an application rather than a download. Time to access is unknown, probably days to weeks.
  - **What I tried:** the ISSDA site refused automated requests (HTTP 403), so I couldn't read the terms.
  - **What to check:** the [ISSDA dataset page](https://www.ucd.ie/issda/data/commissionforenergyregulationcer/) for the form and terms.

## Not recommended, and why

- **Pecan Street Dataport:** the free academic tier covers only 75 homes. More needs a paid licence (from web search).
- **Ausgrid Solar Home data:** 300 solar homes at 30 min over 3 years. It is small and PV-skewed, and a repo note says it may have been removed from Ausgrid's site. Public copies exist on [data.gov.au](https://www.data.gov.au/data/en/dataset/nsw-solar-home-electricty-data).
- **IDEAL Household Energy Dataset:** I recall roughly 255 homes, from memory and not confirmed. That is small for your threshold.
- **Kaggle, Hugging Face and Zenodo re-uploads of the London data:** these are third-party copies. Use the official source.

## What I could not find

- I did not check the documentation of every dataset for the share of households with a complete year. Do this yourself for London, iFlex and SGSC.
- Registry searches on DataCite and Hugging Face sometimes returned malformed responses, so the list may be incomplete. I searched Zenodo, OpenAIRE, DataCite, Hugging Face and PhysioNet, plus the web.
- The London and SGSC access and licence details come from a web search summary, not the registry record.

## Next step

Download GoiEner and London first and build your day-ahead baseline on them. Then validate on ECD-UY. If you want an Irish comparison, send the ISSDA request now, since it's the only one with a wait.

The interactive page is at `/private/tmp/e-qVHPie/home/cwd/dataset-shortlist.html`. I didn't commit anything.

## What I assumed you need

- **R1 (must):** consumption per household, not feeder or grid totals.
- **R2 (must):** at least a year of history per household.
- **R3 (must):** hourly or finer resolution.
- **R4 (nice to have):** downloadable now, without an application.
- **Assumed:** non-commercial research use, and that a few thousand households counts as "hundreds or more".

- **Swiss:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/21398769) was published in July 2026. It covers apartments and houses, including homes with heat pumps, EVs and PV. It has two full years but few published baselines yet.
- **HEAPO:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/15056919) has the longest history, but every home has a heat pump, so demand is strongly weather-driven and unrepresentative.
- **iFlex:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/8248802) includes surveys. It comes from a price-response experiment during COVID, which distorts day-ahead patterns, and it spans only about a year. Check Table 1 of its paper for exact counts.
- **Smart-Grid Smart-City:** the [data.gov.au page](https://data.gov.au/data/dataset/smart-grid-smart-city-customer-trial-data) has half-hourly readings per household. The trial's tariffs and in-home displays may affect behaviour.

For your benchmark, I'd start with GoiEner, London and ECD-UY. They cover three different countries and climates.

## Known, not confirmed

- **Irish CER Smart Metering Trial (ISSDA, UCD):** about 5,000 homes and businesses at 30 min over 2009–2010, about 1.5 years. It is widely used in the literature.
  - **Access:** the search results (not the page) say you complete and sign a request form and email it to ISSDA. That is an application rather than a download. Time to access is unknown, probably days to weeks.
  - **What I tried:** the ISSDA site refused automated requests (HTTP 403), so I couldn't read the terms.
  - **What to check:** the [ISSDA dataset page](https://www.ucd.ie/issda/data/commissionforenergyregulationcer/) for the form and terms.

## Not recommended, and why

- **Pecan Street Dataport:** the free academic tier covers only 75 homes. More needs a paid licence (from web search).
- **Ausgrid Solar Home data:** 300 solar homes at 30 min over 3 years. It is small and PV-skewed, and a repo note says it may have been removed from Ausgrid's site. Public copies exist on [data.gov.au](https://www.data.gov.au/data/en/dataset/nsw-solar-home-electricty-data).
- **IDEAL Household Energy Dataset:** I recall roughly 255 homes, from memory and not confirmed. That is small for your threshold.
- **Kaggle, Hugging Face and Zenodo re-uploads of the London data:** these are third-party copies. Use the official source.

## What I could not find

- I did not check the documentation of every dataset for the share of households with a complete year. Do this yourself for London, iFlex and SGSC.
- Registry searches on DataCite and Hugging Face sometimes returned malformed responses, so the list may be incomplete. I searched Zenodo, OpenAIRE, DataCite, Hugging Face and PhysioNet, plus the web.
- The London and SGSC access and licence details come from a web search summary, not the registry record.

## Next step

Download GoiEner and London first and build your day-ahead baseline on them. Then validate on ECD-UY. If you want an Irish comparison, send the ISSDA request now, since it's the only one with a wait.

The interactive page is at `/private/tmp/e-qVHPie/home/cwd/dataset-shortlist.html`. I didn't commit anything.

## What I assumed you need

- **R1 (must):** consumption per household, not feeder or grid totals.
- **R2 (must):** at least a year of history per household.
- **R3 (must):** hourly or finer resolution.
- **R4 (nice to have):** downloadable now, without an application.
- **Assumed:** non-commercial research use, and that a few thousand households counts as "hundreds or more".

## Shortlist

Short answer: you can download all of these now. None needs an application. Checked 2026-11-12.

| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Households | Resolution / span | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | GoiEner (Spain) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 25,559 supply points | Hourly, about 3 years on average (2014–2022) | Registry record and page, today |
| 2 | Low Carbon London (UK Power Networks) | Open download, London Datastore | CC BY | 5,567 | 30 min, Nov 2011–Feb 2014 | Web search summary; the page itself is not machine-confirmed |
| 3 | ECD-UY (Uruguay) | Open download, figshare | CC0 | 110,953 | 15 min, about 1.8 years | Registry record; the page blocked scripts, so open it in a browser |
| 4 | Swiss residential load profiles | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 2,447 | 15 min, 2023–2024 | Registry record and page, today |
| 5 | HEAPO (Zurich) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | 1,408 | 15 min, Nov 2018–Mar 2024 | Registry record and page, today |
| 6 | iFlex (Norway) | Open download, Zenodo | CC BY 4.0 | About 3,700–4,500 (sources disagree) | Hourly, early 2020–spring 2021 | Registry record and page, today |
| 7 | Smart-Grid Smart-City (Australia) | Open download, data.gov.au | CC BY 3.0 AU | Unclear (about 8,000 to 78,000) | 30 min, 2010–2014 | Web search summary only |

## Details

- **GoiEner:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/7362094) has raw data (15.1 GB) and imputed files for series longer than a year. It is a mix of customers. The `contracted_tariff` field lets you keep households, but the "2.X" tariff also includes small businesses. Zenodo also lists newer versions than the v1 I read. The dataset paper is [Sci. Data 2023](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41597-023-02846-0).
- **London:** the [London Datastore page](https://data.london.gov.uk/dataset/smartmeter-energy-consumption-data-in-london-households-vqm0d) offers a zip of about 765 MB. Households join and leave at different times, so filter for those with a full year. This is the most-used household benchmark, and one day-ahead study on it is [Benchmarking of Load Forecasting Methods Using Residential Smart Meter Data](https://doi.org/10.3390/app12199844). Use this official copy rather than the Kaggle or GitHub mirrors.
- **ECD-UY:** by far the largest. The [figshare record](https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.16965400) is CC0, and its paper is [Sci. Data 2022](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41597-022-01122-x). It is a southern-hemisphere climate with little weather metadata.
- **Swiss:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/21398769) was published in July 2026. It covers apartments and houses, including homes with heat pumps, EVs and PV. It has two full years but few published baselines yet.
- **HEAPO:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/15056919) has the longest history, but every home has a heat pump, so demand is strongly weather-driven and unrepresentative.
- **iFlex:** the [Zenodo record](https://zenodo.org/records/8248802) includes surveys. It comes from a price-response experiment during COVID, which distorts day-ahead patterns, and it spans only about a year. Check Table 1 of its paper for exact counts.
- **Smart-Grid Smart-City:** the [data.gov.au page](https://data.gov.au/data/dataset/smart-grid-smart-city-customer-trial-data) has half-hourly readings per household. The trial's tariffs and in-home displays may affect behaviour.

For your benchmark, I'd start with GoiEner, London and ECD-UY. They cover three different countries and climates.

- The London and SGSC access and licence details come from a web search summary, not the registry record.

## Next step

Download GoiEner and London first and build your day-ahead baseline on them. Then validate on ECD-UY. If you want an Irish comparison, send the ISSDA request now, since it's the only one with a wait.

The interactive page is at `/private/tmp/e-qVHPie/home/cwd/dataset-shortlist.html`. I didn't commit anything.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I'm answering from memory and didn't check the current links or licenses, so confirm those on each landing page. Here are the datasets that fit your criteria (individual households, hundreds or more, at least a year, hourly or finer):

| Dataset | Households | Resolution | Span | Access |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **GoiEner** (Basque Country, Spain) | about 25,000 | hourly | several years | **Direct download** from Zenodo (CC-BY). It's the largest open option and has metadata such as tariff and postcode. |
| **London Smart Meters** (UK Power Networks, Low Carbon London) | about 5,500 | 30 min | Nov 2011 to Feb 2014 | **Direct download** from the London Datastore and Kaggle. About 1,100 households were on a dynamic time-of-use tariff, which you'd need to handle. Many households have gaps or start late. |
| **Ausgrid Solar Home** (Sydney) | 300 | 30 min | 3 years | **Direct download** from Ausgrid's site. All homes have rooftop PV, so net and gross load differ, which matters for forecasting. |
| **Smart Grid Smart City** (Australia) | tens of thousands | 30 min | about 2012 to 2014 | **Direct download** from data.gov.au. The data is messy, and coverage varies by customer. |
| **CER Irish Smart Metering Trial** | about 4,000 homes (plus about 1,000 SMEs) | 30 min | about 1.5 years (2009 to 2010) | **Application required** through ISSDA. It's free for research, but you sign a data-use agreement, and approval can take days. |
| **Pecan Street Dataport** (US) | hundreds to thousands | 1 min to 15 min, including circuit-level | varies by home | **Application required**. Academic access is free, and commercial use is paid. A small sample is open. |

**Practical notes**
- **Fastest start:** GoiEner is the best match for your criteria and needs no application. London is the most widely used benchmark, so it gives you the most published results to compare against. The CER data is a common benchmark but requires an application.
- **Datasets that look like matches but aren't:**
  - The UCI *ElectricityLoadDiagrams* data (370 clients, Portugal) is mostly commercial and industrial customers, not homes.
  - REFIT, UK-DALE and Smart* have only about 5 to 20 homes each.
  - ResStock and Buildings-900K are simulated, not measured.
- **Weather and calendar features:** none of these ship with weather data. You'll need to join it yourself, for example from ERA5, Met Office or Bureau of Meteorology data. Day-ahead benchmarks usually need temperature and holiday calendars.
- **Representativeness:** the datasets cover different countries, years and tariffs, and Ausgrid is PV-only. Using two or three of them would make your benchmark more robust.

I can check the current download links and licenses, or write a loader that puts these into one common schema.

| Dataset | Households | Resolution | History | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **GoiEner** (Spain, Basque Country and Navarre, on Zenodo) | ~25,000 | Hourly | Several years (roughly 2014–2022) | Largest and most recent open set. It has per-customer metadata such as tariff and location, which lets you pull weather. |
| **London Smart Meters** (UK Power Networks, London Datastore, mirrored on Kaggle) | ~5,500 | 30 min | Nov 2011 – Feb 2014 | Includes a weather file and ACORN demographic groups. Coverage varies by household, so filter for those with at least a year. |
| **Ausgrid Solar Home** (Australia) | 300 | 30 min | 3 years | All homes have rooftop PV, so it suits net-load forecasting more than plain demand. |
| **IDEAL** (UK, Edinburgh DataShare) | 255 | 1 s to 1 min | Up to ~2 years | Many homes have shorter series, and you'd have to aggregate to hourly. |
| **Smart-Grid Smart-City** (Australia, data.gov.au) | Thousands | 30 min | ~2010–2014 | The format is messier, so it needs more cleaning. |

## Free, but you have to apply

| Dataset | Households | Resolution | History | Access |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **CER Smart Metering Project** (Ireland, via ISSDA) | ~4,200 residential, plus SMEs | 30 min | ~1.5 years (2009–2010) | Sign a request form and agree to the terms. Approval usually takes days. It has no location data, so you can't join weather. |
| **SERL** (UK Smart Energy Research Lab, via UK Data Service) | 13,000+ | 30 min | Multi-year, recent | Requires registration and project approval. Some tiers need a secure-access setup. |
| **Pecan Street Dataport** (Texas) | ~1,000+ | 1 to 15 min, with circuit-level data | Multi-year | Free for academics through a university agreement. Commercial use is paid. |

## Things to watch

- **Avoid the UCI "ElectricityLoadDiagrams 2011–2014" set.** It has 370 clients, but they're mostly commercial and industrial, and it's often mislabelled as households.
- **NREL ResStock/ComStock and similar sets are simulated, not metered.** They're fine for stress tests but not for a real-world benchmark.
- **REFIT, UK-DALE and Smart\*** have only a handful of homes, so they're too small for your criteria.
- **Check the year-long history per household.** Many datasets have staggered start and end dates, so you'll need to filter.
- **Day-ahead forecasting needs exogenous data.** You'll want weather and a holiday calendar. GoiEner and London support this best, and CER doesn't.

For a start without paperwork, I'd use GoiEner as the main benchmark and London as a second dataset for cross-checking. If you can wait a few days, adding CER or SERL would give you a more widely cited baseline for comparison.

I can download and profile any of these, for example counting households with at least a year of data and checking for gaps, if you want.

| **Ausgrid Solar Home** (Australia) | 300 | 30 min | 3 years | All homes have rooftop PV, so it suits net-load forecasting more than plain demand. |
| **IDEAL** (UK, Edinburgh DataShare) | 255 | 1 s to 1 min | Up to ~2 years | Many homes have shorter series, and you'd have to aggregate to hourly. |
| **Smart-Grid Smart-City** (Australia, data.gov.au) | Thousands | 30 min | ~2010–2014 | The format is messier, so it needs more cleaning. |

## Free, but you have to apply

| Dataset | Households | Resolution | History | Access |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **CER Smart Metering Project** (Ireland, via ISSDA) | ~4,200 residential, plus SMEs | 30 min | ~1.5 years (2009–2010) | Sign a request form and agree to the terms. Approval usually takes days. It has no location data, so you can't join weather. |
| **SERL** (UK Smart Energy Research Lab, via UK Data Service) | 13,000+ | 30 min | Multi-year, recent | Requires registration and project approval. Some tiers need a secure-access setup. |
| **Pecan Street Dataport** (Texas) | ~1,000+ | 1 to 15 min, with circuit-level data | Multi-year | Free for academics through a university agreement. Commercial use is paid. |

## Things to watch

- **Avoid the UCI "ElectricityLoadDiagrams 2011–2014" set.** It has 370 clients, but they're mostly commercial and industrial, and it's often mislabelled as households.
- **NREL ResStock/ComStock and similar sets are simulated, not metered.** They're fine for stress tests but not for a real-world benchmark.
- **REFIT, UK-DALE and Smart\*** have only a handful of homes, so they're too small for your criteria.
- **Check the year-long history per household.** Many datasets have staggered start and end dates, so you'll need to filter.
- **Day-ahead forecasting needs exogenous data.** You'll want weather and a holiday calendar. GoiEner and London support this best, and CER doesn't.

For a start without paperwork, I'd use GoiEner as the main benchmark and London as a second dataset for cross-checking. If you can wait a few days, adding CER or SERL would give you a more widely cited baseline for comparison.

I can download and profile any of these, for example counting households with at least a year of data and checking for gaps, if you want.
Prompt

I want to benchmark day-ahead electricity demand forecasting for individual homes, so I need smart-meter consumption data per household, not feeder or grid totals. Ideally hundreds of households or more, at least a year of history each, at hourly resolution or finer. What public datasets are there, and can I just download them or do I have to apply?

00-skill-firedtool_used
tool"Skill"
input_match"\"skill\"\\s*:\\s*\"(?:[\\w-]+:)?dataset-scout\""
01-recall-low-carbon-londonllm

PASS if the final answer recommends or lists the London smart meter dataset from the Low Carbon London project (also known as "SmartMeter Energy Consumption Data in London Households", LCL, the UK Power Networks London smart meter data on the London Datastore, or the Kaggle "Smart meters in London" copy) as a candidate household electricity dataset. FAIL if the answer does not mention it, or mentions it only to say it is unsuitable.

02-recall-cer-irish-smart-meterllm

PASS if the final answer recommends or lists the Irish CER Smart Metering Project electricity data (also known as the CER Electricity Customer Behaviour Trial 2009-2010, the Irish Commission for Energy Regulation smart meter data, or the ISSDA smart meter data) as a candidate household electricity dataset. FAIL if the answer does not mention it, or mentions it only to say it is unsuitable.

03-recall-smart-grid-smart-cityllm

PASS if the final answer recommends or lists the Smart-Grid Smart-City Customer Trial Data (also known as SGSC, the Ausgrid-led Smart Grid Smart City trial from New South Wales, Australia, published on data.gov.au) as a candidate household electricity dataset. FAIL if the answer does not mention it, or mentions it only to say it is unsuitable.

04-recall-pecan-streetllm

PASS if the final answer recommends or lists Pecan Street Dataport (also known as Pecan Street, the Pecan Street Inc. residential energy data from Austin, Texas and other testbeds) as a candidate household electricity dataset. FAIL if the answer does not mention it, or mentions it only to say it is unsuitable.

05-access-low-carbon-london-openllm

PASS if the final answer states that the Low Carbon London smart meter data ("SmartMeter Energy Consumption Data in London Households", LCL) can be downloaded directly, without an application, from the London Datastore. If the answer names its licence, the licence must be Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY, version 4.0 on the London Datastore page). FAIL if the answer does not mention this dataset, says it must be applied for, or gives a different licence (for example a non-commercial one).

06-access-cer-issda-requestllm

PASS if the final answer states that the Irish CER Smart Metering Project electricity data is not an open download but must be requested from the Irish Social Science Data Archive (ISSDA), for example by submitting an ISSDA data request form that is reviewed before access is granted, and that it is provided for non-commercial research or teaching. Mentioning the ISSDA end user licence, or that ISSDA supplies data only for use in the EEA and countries with an EU adequacy decision, is welcome but not required. FAIL if the answer does not mention the CER data, describes it as freely downloadable, or says nothing about how access to it is obtained.

07-access-pecan-street-free-tier-limitedllm

PASS if the final answer states that Pecan Street Dataport requires an account and a licence, and that free access for university researchers covers only a limited subset of homes (75 homes: 25 each from Austin, New York and California) while the full datasets require a paid licence. An answer that conveys either the limited free subset or the paid licence for the full data passes. FAIL if the answer does not mention Pecan Street, describes the full dataset as freely downloadable or free for all academic use without any limitation, or says nothing about how access to it is obtained.

08-access-smart-grid-smart-city-openllm

PASS if the final answer states that the Smart-Grid Smart-City (SGSC) Customer Trial Data can be downloaded directly, without an application, from data.gov.au. If the answer names its licence, the licence must be Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY 3.0 Australia). FAIL if the answer does not mention this dataset, says it must be applied for, or gives a different licence.

09-trap-uci-individual-householdllm

PASS if the final answer does not present the UCI "Individual household electric power consumption" dataset (one-minute measurements from a single house in Sceaux near Paris, December 2006 to November 2010) as meeting the requirement of many households. Not mentioning it passes. Mentioning it while saying that it covers only one household passes. FAIL if the answer recommends or lists this dataset as a multi-household dataset, or lists it as a candidate for this many-household benchmark without saying that it contains a single household.

10-long-tailllm

PASS if the final answer recommends or lists at least one of these less widely known household electricity datasets: the IDEAL household energy dataset (255 homes in and around Edinburgh, Scotland, on Edinburgh DataShare); or the GoiEner smart meter data (hourly consumption from customers of the Spanish electricity cooperative GoiEner, on Zenodo). FAIL if the answer mentions neither of them.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
## What I assumed you need
- **R1 (must):** adult ICU time series of routine vitals and labs.
- **R2 (must):** a time-stamped sepsis onset label (Sepsis-3 or close), or data you can derive one from.
- **R3 (must):** thousands of patients in a clear setting.
- **R4 (nice to have):** data you can get within about a week.

I checked everything against PhysioNet's registry and pages on 2026-11-11/12. I marked anything I didn't re-read today as "memory".

## Shortlist
| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Size | Fit | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2019, sepsis | **Open download, minutes** | CC-BY-4.0 | 40,336 ICU stays | Strong; label included | Today |
| 2 | MIMIC-IV v3.1 | Credentialed, days to weeks | PhysioNet Credentialed Health Data License | Large (single centre) | Strong; you derive the label | Today |
| 3 | eICU-CRD v2.0 | Credentialed, same route as MIMIC-IV | PhysioNet Credentialed Health Data License | Multi-centre US | Strong; you derive the label | Today |
| 4 | HiRID v1.1.1 | Credentialed plus author approval, weeks | PhysioNet Contributor Review Health Data License | ~34k admissions | Strong; you derive the label | Today |
| 5 | SICdb v1.0.8 | Credentialed plus contributor approval, weeks | PhysioNet Contributor Review Health Data License | Not checked | Partial; label unclear | Today |
| 6 | AmsterdamUMCdb | Application, DANS licence | DANS Licence | ~23k admissions (memory) | Strong if the Sepsis-3 code works | Registry record only; access terms from memory |

## Details
**1. Challenge 2019 (start here).** Link: https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2019/1.0.0/
- It has hourly vitals and labs plus an hourly sepsis label, so you can run a baseline this week.
- The label is a challenge convention, not a charted event. From memory, onset is set 6 hours before clinical suspicion of infection, using Sepsis-3 criteria.
- It has only about 40 variables, with shifted times. You can't re-derive onset from source tables, and the hidden test set was never released.
- Don't use the Hugging Face copies. They are unofficial.

**2–5. PhysioNet credentialed datasets.** All four share one route:
1. Create a PhysioNet account.
2. Complete the CITI "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. Per PhysioNet's instructions, affiliate with "Massachusetts Institute of Technology Affiliates" and upload the training report, not the certificate.
3. Submit the credentialing application.
4. Sign the data use agreement for each dataset.

- PhysioNet's registry text says only credentialed users who sign the agreement can access the files.
- Access is personal, so each person on your team applies separately.
- One credentialing covers all of these datasets, but each needs its own agreement.
- HiRID and SICdb add a step. You submit a specific research question and the authors or contributors approve it. The pages say this, so budget extra time for it.
- None of these has a Sepsis-3 column. You derive onset from SOFA plus suspected infection (antibiotic and culture timing). MIMIC-IV is the easiest for this, because it has well-documented antibiotic and culture tables and open community code (from memory).
- eICU has a demo (100 stays, open, ODbL licence) at https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd-demo/2.0.1/. MIMIC-IV has one too at https://physionet.org/content/mimic-iv-demo/2.2/. You can build your pipeline on these while you wait.
- Don't paste restricted records into hosted AI tools. PhysioNet's 24 Sept 2025 policy prohibits it unless zero retention and no training are verified.

**6. AmsterdamUMCdb.** Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The registry lists it as application-based under the DANS Licence.
- A GigaDB record titled "Sepsis-3 criteria in AmsterdamUMCdb: open-source code implementation" exists (https://doi.org/10.5524/102204).
- The landing and access pages wouldn't load, so the process is from memory: a request plus a signed licence agreement. Confirm it before planning around it.

## Known, not confirmed
- **AmsterdamUMCdb access steps and timing.** I know the registry says "application". I tried the DOI, the DataCite API and GitHub, and all failed with connection errors. Check the DOI page.
- **SICdb sepsis fit.** The page lists sepsis detection as an application area. I didn't verify antibiotic or culture data, which you'd need to derive onset. Check https://www.sicdb.com/Documentation/.
- **Challenge 2019 label definition.** The 6-hour rule is from memory. Check it against the challenge paper.

## Not recommended, and why
- **PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2012.** It predicts mortality only.
- **GOSSIS-1 / WiDS 2020.** These are severity-score summaries, not time series with onset.
- **Zigong infection ICU database.** It is a single-hospital infection cohort. I didn't check it for onset labels or time series, and it needs credentialing.
- **Zenodo sepsis records.** These are small clinical-study supplements or unrelated records, not ICU time series.

## What I could not find
- **Literature evidence of use.** The paper search returned almost nothing, so I have no verified papers to cite for any dataset. Search for the 2019 challenge's official results paper and for HiRID benchmark papers yourself.
- **Newer derived datasets.** I saw MIMIC-IV-Ext-CLIF in the results but didn't evaluate it. I also didn't look for pre-built Sepsis-3 tables.

## Next step
1. Today, download Challenge 2019 and build the baseline.
2. Today, start the CITI course and the PhysioNet credentialing application. Ask your supervisor for the reference or sign-off the form needs.
3. In parallel, develop your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo, then run it on full MIMIC-IV once approved.
4. Use eICU or HiRID later for external validation.

I can't say how long credentialing will take from this check. Confirm the current review times on PhysioNet.

- eICU has a demo (100 stays, open, ODbL licence) at https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd-demo/2.0.1/. MIMIC-IV has one too at https://physionet.org/content/mimic-iv-demo/2.2/. You can build your pipeline on these while you wait.
- Don't paste restricted records into hosted AI tools. PhysioNet's 24 Sept 2025 policy prohibits it unless zero retention and no training are verified.

**6. AmsterdamUMCdb.** Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The registry lists it as application-based under the DANS Licence.
- A GigaDB record titled "Sepsis-3 criteria in AmsterdamUMCdb: open-source code implementation" exists (https://doi.org/10.5524/102204).
- The landing and access pages wouldn't load, so the process is from memory: a request plus a signed licence agreement. Confirm it before planning around it.

## Known, not confirmed
- **AmsterdamUMCdb access steps and timing.** I know the registry says "application". I tried the DOI, the DataCite API and GitHub, and all failed with connection errors. Check the DOI page.
- **SICdb sepsis fit.** The page lists sepsis detection as an application area. I didn't verify antibiotic or culture data, which you'd need to derive onset. Check https://www.sicdb.com/Documentation/.
- **Challenge 2019 label definition.** The 6-hour rule is from memory. Check it against the challenge paper.

## Not recommended, and why
- **PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2012.** It predicts mortality only.
- **GOSSIS-1 / WiDS 2020.** These are severity-score summaries, not time series with onset.
- **Zigong infection ICU database.** It is a single-hospital infection cohort. I didn't check it for onset labels or time series, and it needs credentialing.
- **Zenodo sepsis records.** These are small clinical-study supplements or unrelated records, not ICU time series.

## What I could not find
- **Literature evidence of use.** The paper search returned almost nothing, so I have no verified papers to cite for any dataset. Search for the 2019 challenge's official results paper and for HiRID benchmark papers yourself.
- **Newer derived datasets.** I saw MIMIC-IV-Ext-CLIF in the results but didn't evaluate it. I also didn't look for pre-built Sepsis-3 tables.

## Next step
1. Today, download Challenge 2019 and build the baseline.
2. Today, start the CITI course and the PhysioNet credentialing application. Ask your supervisor for the reference or sign-off the form needs.
3. In parallel, develop your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo, then run it on full MIMIC-IV once approved.
4. Use eICU or HiRID later for external validation.

I can't say how long credentialing will take from this check. Confirm the current review times on PhysioNet.

## What I assumed you need
- **R1 (must):** adult ICU time series of routine vitals and labs.
- **R2 (must):** a time-stamped sepsis onset label (Sepsis-3 or close), or data you can derive one from.
- **R3 (must):** thousands of patients in a clear setting.

## Shortlist
| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Size | Fit | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2019, sepsis | **Open download, minutes** | CC-BY-4.0 | 40,336 ICU stays | Strong; label included | Today |
| 2 | MIMIC-IV v3.1 | Credentialed, days to weeks | PhysioNet Credentialed Health Data License | Large (single centre) | Strong; you derive the label | Today |

## Details
**1. Challenge 2019 (start here).** Link: https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2019/1.0.0/
- It has hourly vitals and labs plus an hourly sepsis label, so you can run a baseline this week.
- The label is a challenge convention, not a charted event. From memory, onset is set 6 hours before clinical suspicion of infection, using Sepsis-3 criteria.
- It has only about 40 variables, with shifted times. You can't re-derive onset from source tables, and the hidden test set was never released.
- Don't use the Hugging Face copies. They are unofficial.

**2–5. PhysioNet credentialed datasets.** All four share one route:
1. Create a PhysioNet account.
2. Complete the CITI "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. Per PhysioNet's instructions, affiliate with "Massachusetts Institute of Technology Affiliates" and upload the training report, not the certificate.
3. Submit the credentialing application.
4. Sign the data use agreement for each dataset.

- PhysioNet's registry text says only credentialed users who sign the agreement can access the files.
- Access is personal, so each person on your team applies separately.
- One credentialing covers all of these datasets, but each needs its own agreement.
- HiRID and SICdb add a step. You submit a specific research question and the authors or contributors approve it. The pages say this, so budget extra time for it.
- None of these has a Sepsis-3 column. You derive onset from SOFA plus suspected infection (antibiotic and culture timing). MIMIC-IV is the easiest for this, because it has well-documented antibiotic and culture tables and open community code (from memory).
- eICU has a demo (100 stays, open, ODbL licence) at https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd-demo/2.0.1/. MIMIC-IV has one too at https://physionet.org/content/mimic-iv-demo/2.2/. You can build your pipeline on these while you wait.
- Don't paste restricted records into hosted AI tools. PhysioNet's 24 Sept 2025 policy prohibits it unless zero retention and no training are verified.

**6. AmsterdamUMCdb.** Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The registry lists it as application-based under the DANS Licence.

## Details
**1. Challenge 2019 (start here).** Link: https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2019/1.0.0/
- It has hourly vitals and labs plus an hourly sepsis label, so you can run a baseline this week.
- The label is a challenge convention, not a charted event. From memory, onset is set 6 hours before clinical suspicion of infection, using Sepsis-3 criteria.
- It has only about 40 variables, with shifted times. You can't re-derive onset from source tables, and the hidden test set was never released.
- Don't use the Hugging Face copies. They are unofficial.

**2–5. PhysioNet credentialed datasets.** All four share one route:
1. Create a PhysioNet account.
2. Complete the CITI "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. Per PhysioNet's instructions, affiliate with "Massachusetts Institute of Technology Affiliates" and upload the training report, not the certificate.
3. Submit the credentialing application.
4. Sign the data use agreement for each dataset.

- PhysioNet's registry text says only credentialed users who sign the agreement can access the files.
- Access is personal, so each person on your team applies separately.
- One credentialing covers all of these datasets, but each needs its own agreement.
- HiRID and SICdb add a step. You submit a specific research question and the authors or contributors approve it. The pages say this, so budget extra time for it.
- None of these has a Sepsis-3 column. You derive onset from SOFA plus suspected infection (antibiotic and culture timing). MIMIC-IV is the easiest for this, because it has well-documented antibiotic and culture tables and open community code (from memory).
- eICU has a demo (100 stays, open, ODbL licence) at https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd-demo/2.0.1/. MIMIC-IV has one too at https://physionet.org/content/mimic-iv-demo/2.2/. You can build your pipeline on these while you wait.
- Don't paste restricted records into hosted AI tools. PhysioNet's 24 Sept 2025 policy prohibits it unless zero retention and no training are verified.

**6. AmsterdamUMCdb.** Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The registry lists it as application-based under the DANS Licence.
- A GigaDB record titled "Sepsis-3 criteria in AmsterdamUMCdb: open-source code implementation" exists (https://doi.org/10.5524/102204).
- The landing and access pages wouldn't load, so the process is from memory: a request plus a signed licence agreement. Confirm it before planning around it.

## Known, not confirmed
- **AmsterdamUMCdb access steps and timing.** I know the registry says "application". I tried the DOI, the DataCite API and GitHub, and all failed with connection errors. Check the DOI page.
- **SICdb sepsis fit.** The page lists sepsis detection as an application area. I didn't verify antibiotic or culture data, which you'd need to derive onset. Check https://www.sicdb.com/Documentation/.
- **Challenge 2019 label definition.** The 6-hour rule is from memory. Check it against the challenge paper.

## Not recommended, and why
- **PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2012.** It predicts mortality only.
- **GOSSIS-1 / WiDS 2020.** These are severity-score summaries, not time series with onset.
- **Zigong infection ICU database.** It is a single-hospital infection cohort. I didn't check it for onset labels or time series, and it needs credentialing.
- **Zenodo sepsis records.** These are small clinical-study supplements or unrelated records, not ICU time series.

## What I could not find
- **Literature evidence of use.** The paper search returned almost nothing, so I have no verified papers to cite for any dataset. Search for the 2019 challenge's official results paper and for HiRID benchmark papers yourself.
- **Newer derived datasets.** I saw MIMIC-IV-Ext-CLIF in the results but didn't evaluate it. I also didn't look for pre-built Sepsis-3 tables.

## Next step
1. Today, download Challenge 2019 and build the baseline.
2. Today, start the CITI course and the PhysioNet credentialing application. Ask your supervisor for the reference or sign-off the form needs.
3. In parallel, develop your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo, then run it on full MIMIC-IV once approved.
4. Use eICU or HiRID later for external validation.

I can't say how long credentialing will take from this check. Confirm the current review times on PhysioNet.

## What I assumed you need

- **R1 (must):** adult ICU time series of vitals and labs.
- **R2 (must):** time-stamped sepsis onset, or enough data to derive Sepsis-3 onset yourself. That means SOFA inputs plus antibiotic and culture timestamps.
- **R3 (nice):** enough patients, ideally from more than one site.
- **R4 (nice):** obtainable within a week.

I checked these on 2026-11-11 against PhysioNet's registry and pages. Items marked "from memory" were not confirmed today.

## Shortlist

| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Size | Fit | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2019 (early sepsis prediction) | **Open download, minutes** | CC-BY-4.0 | 40,336 patients, 42 MB | Strong | today |
| 2 | MIMIC-IV v3.1 | Credentialed: CITI training and DUA, about 1–2 weeks | PhysioNet Credentialed Health Data License | tens of thousands of ICU stays | Strong (derive onset) | access today, contents from memory |
| 3 | eICU-CRD | Credentialed, same application as MIMIC-IV | PhysioNet Credentialed | about 200k stays | Strong (derive onset) | access today, contents from memory |
| 4 | HiRID (Bern) | Credentialed plus authors' approval of your research question, weeks | PhysioNet Contributor Review | about 34k admissions | Strong (derive onset) | today |
| 5 | AmsterdamUMCdb v1.0.2 | Signed access and licence form via DANS, weeks | DANS Licence | tens of thousands of admissions | Strong (derive onset) | access today |
| 6 | SICdb (Salzburg) | Credentialed plus contributor approval, weeks | PhysioNet Contributor Review | not checked | Partial | today |
| 7 | MIMIC-IV demo / eICU demo | Open, minutes | ODbL-1.0 | about 100 / about 2,500 stays (from memory) | Pipeline testing only | today |

## Details

**1. Challenge 2019: start here today.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2019/
- The page states there is one hourly file per subject. It has two hospital systems (sets A and B, 20,336 and 20,000 subjects).
- Labels come from Sepsis-3, and the page says they are "shifted… ahead by six hours", so the task is already 6-hour-early prediction.
- Download needs no credentials.
- Caveats:
  - It has a fixed set of about 40 variables, from memory.
  - You can't re-derive the label.
  - The challenge's hidden test set isn't included.
- Official scoring code and a utility function that rewards early predictions are provided.

**2–3. MIMIC-IV and eICU: the main research data.**
- Links: https://physionet.org/content/mimiciv/ and https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd/
- Neither has an onset column. You derive Sepsis-3 from SOFA plus suspected infection (antibiotics and cultures). Community derivation code exists, from memory.
- Access steps, confirmed on the pages:
  1. Create a PhysioNet account.
  2. Complete CITI's "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. PhysioNet says to affiliate with "Massachusetts Institute of Technology Affiliates" and not register as an independent learner, which avoids extra fees.
  3. Upload the CITI training report, not the certificate.
  4. Apply for credentialing. A reference may be requested, from memory.
  5. Sign the data use agreement.
- Access is personal, so your supervisor and lab mates each apply separately.
- Don't send this data to hosted LLM services. PhysioNet's rule is at https://physionet.org/news/post/llm-responsible-use/.
- MIMIC-IV is single-centre. eICU is multi-centre and is the natural external-validation set.

**4. HiRID.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/hirid/
- It has roughly 2-minute resolution from a Swiss ICU, which suits time-series modelling.
- The page says you must be credentialed, sign the DUA "and provide a specific research question". The data authors approve the request, so timing is less predictable.

**5. AmsterdamUMCdb.**
- Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The record is restricted and points to a signed access request and end-use licence form (details at github.com/AmsterdamUMC/AmsterdamUMCdb). Whether the form needs an institutional signature is from memory, so check it.
- A DataCite record titled "Sepsis-3 criteria in AmsterdamUMCdb: open-source code implementation" exists, which could save you derivation work.

**6. SICdb.** The page says contributor approval is required, along with PhysioNet credentialing and a research question. It is a possible later validation site.

**7. Demos.** The open MIMIC-IV and eICU samples are too small to model. Use them to write and test your Sepsis-3 pipeline while credentialing is pending.

## Known, not confirmed

- **Paper citations:** the literature APIs returned almost nothing, so I'm citing no papers. Check the Challenge 2019 paper (Reyna et al., *Critical Care Medicine*), which the page says is the official one.
- **Contents of MIMIC-IV, eICU and AmsterdamUMCdb:** I didn't read the variable lists today. Sizes and derivation code availability are from memory.
- **Credentialing timing:** "about 1–2 weeks" is from memory.

## Not recommended, and why

- **Hugging Face copies of the Challenge data:** unofficial mirrors. Use PhysioNet.
- **Zenodo and OpenAIRE sepsis supplements:** these have mortality, biomarker or single-study tables, with no hourly vitals or onset times.
- **MIMIC-style datasets with only discharge codes:** ICD-coded sepsis gives no onset time.

## What I could not find

- An open, multi-centre dataset with raw Sepsis-3 inputs and no application.
- Working literature search results this session.
- The old Amsterdam URL (amsterdammedicaldatascience.nl/amsterdamumcdb/) returned 404, so use the DOI link.

## Next step

1. Today: download Challenge 2019 and run a baseline against its utility score.
2. Today: start the CITI course and the PhysioNet credentialing application. That is the slow part.
3. While you wait: build your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo.
4. After approval: train on MIMIC-IV and validate externally on eICU. Ask the HiRID authors in parallel.

Interactive page (filter, compare, export an access plan): `/private/tmp/e-upQ7Yk/home/cwd/dataset-shortlist.html`

3. While you wait: build your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo.
4. After approval: train on MIMIC-IV and validate externally on eICU. Ask the HiRID authors in parallel.

## What I assumed you need

- **R1 (must):** adult ICU time series of vitals and labs.
- **R2 (must):** time-stamped sepsis onset, or enough data to derive Sepsis-3 onset yourself. That means SOFA inputs plus antibiotic and culture timestamps.
- **R3 (nice):** enough patients, ideally from more than one site.
- **R4 (nice):** obtainable within a week.

I checked these on 2026-11-11 against PhysioNet's registry and pages. Items marked "from memory" were not confirmed today.

## Shortlist

| # | Dataset | Access | Licence | Size | Fit | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2019 (early sepsis prediction) | **Open download, minutes** | CC-BY-4.0 | 40,336 patients, 42 MB | Strong | today |
| 2 | MIMIC-IV v3.1 | Credentialed: CITI training and DUA, about 1–2 weeks | PhysioNet Credentialed Health Data License | tens of thousands of ICU stays | Strong (derive onset) | access today, contents from memory |
| 3 | eICU-CRD | Credentialed, same application as MIMIC-IV | PhysioNet Credentialed | about 200k stays | Strong (derive onset) | access today, contents from memory |
| 4 | HiRID (Bern) | Credentialed plus authors' approval of your research question, weeks | PhysioNet Contributor Review | about 34k admissions | Strong (derive onset) | today |
| 5 | AmsterdamUMCdb v1.0.2 | Signed access and licence form via DANS, weeks | DANS Licence | tens of thousands of admissions | Strong (derive onset) | access today |
| 6 | SICdb (Salzburg) | Credentialed plus contributor approval, weeks | PhysioNet Contributor Review | not checked | Partial | today |
| 7 | MIMIC-IV demo / eICU demo | Open, minutes | ODbL-1.0 | about 100 / about 2,500 stays (from memory) | Pipeline testing only | today |

## Details

**1. Challenge 2019: start here today.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2019/
- The page states there is one hourly file per subject. It has two hospital systems (sets A and B, 20,336 and 20,000 subjects).
- Labels come from Sepsis-3, and the page says they are "shifted… ahead by six hours", so the task is already 6-hour-early prediction.
- Download needs no credentials.
- Caveats:
  - It has a fixed set of about 40 variables, from memory.
  - You can't re-derive the label.
  - The challenge's hidden test set isn't included.
- Official scoring code and a utility function that rewards early predictions are provided.

**2–3. MIMIC-IV and eICU: the main research data.**
- Links: https://physionet.org/content/mimiciv/ and https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd/
- Neither has an onset column. You derive Sepsis-3 from SOFA plus suspected infection (antibiotics and cultures). Community derivation code exists, from memory.
- Access steps, confirmed on the pages:
  1. Create a PhysioNet account.
  2. Complete CITI's "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. PhysioNet says to affiliate with "Massachusetts Institute of Technology Affiliates" and not register as an independent learner, which avoids extra fees.
  3. Upload the CITI training report, not the certificate.
  4. Apply for credentialing. A reference may be requested, from memory.
  5. Sign the data use agreement.
- Access is personal, so your supervisor and lab mates each apply separately.
- Don't send this data to hosted LLM services. PhysioNet's rule is at https://physionet.org/news/post/llm-responsible-use/.
- MIMIC-IV is single-centre. eICU is multi-centre and is the natural external-validation set.

**4. HiRID.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/hirid/
- It has roughly 2-minute resolution from a Swiss ICU, which suits time-series modelling.
- The page says you must be credentialed, sign the DUA "and provide a specific research question". The data authors approve the request, so timing is less predictable.

**5. AmsterdamUMCdb.**
- Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The record is restricted and points to a signed access request and end-use licence form (details at github.com/AmsterdamUMC/AmsterdamUMCdb). Whether the form needs an institutional signature is from memory, so check it.
- A DataCite record titled "Sepsis-3 criteria in AmsterdamUMCdb: open-source code implementation" exists, which could save you derivation work.

**6. SICdb.** The page says contributor approval is required, along with PhysioNet credentialing and a research question. It is a possible later validation site.

**7. Demos.** The open MIMIC-IV and eICU samples are too small to model. Use them to write and test your Sepsis-3 pipeline while credentialing is pending.

## Known, not confirmed

- **Paper citations:** the literature APIs returned almost nothing, so I'm citing no papers. Check the Challenge 2019 paper (Reyna et al., *Critical Care Medicine*), which the page says is the official one.
- **Contents of MIMIC-IV, eICU and AmsterdamUMCdb:** I didn't read the variable lists today. Sizes and derivation code availability are from memory.
- **Credentialing timing:** "about 1–2 weeks" is from memory.

## Not recommended, and why

- **Hugging Face copies of the Challenge data:** unofficial mirrors. Use PhysioNet.
- **Zenodo and OpenAIRE sepsis supplements:** these have mortality, biomarker or single-study tables, with no hourly vitals or onset times.
- **MIMIC-style datasets with only discharge codes:** ICD-coded sepsis gives no onset time.

## What I could not find

- An open, multi-centre dataset with raw Sepsis-3 inputs and no application.
- Working literature search results this session.
- The old Amsterdam URL (amsterdammedicaldatascience.nl/amsterdamumcdb/) returned 404, so use the DOI link.

## Next step

1. Today: download Challenge 2019 and run a baseline against its utility score.
2. Today: start the CITI course and the PhysioNet credentialing application. That is the slow part.
3. While you wait: build your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo.
4. After approval: train on MIMIC-IV and validate externally on eICU. Ask the HiRID authors in parallel.

  5. Sign the data use agreement.
- Access is personal, so your supervisor and lab mates each apply separately.
- Don't send this data to hosted LLM services. PhysioNet's rule is at https://physionet.org/news/post/llm-responsible-use/.
- MIMIC-IV is single-centre. eICU is multi-centre and is the natural external-validation set.

**4. HiRID.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/hirid/
- It has roughly 2-minute resolution from a Swiss ICU, which suits time-series modelling.
- The page says you must be credentialed, sign the DUA "and provide a specific research question". The data authors approve the request, so timing is less predictable.

**5. AmsterdamUMCdb.**
- Link: https://doi.org/10.17026/dans-22u-f8vd
- The record is restricted and points to a signed access request and end-use licence form (details at github.com/AmsterdamUMC/AmsterdamUMCdb). Whether the form needs an institutional signature is from memory, so check it.
- A DataCite record titled "Sepsis-3 criteria in AmsterdamUMCdb: open-source code implementation" exists, which could save you derivation work.

**6. SICdb.** The page says contributor approval is required, along with PhysioNet credentialing and a research question. It is a possible later validation site.

**7. Demos.** The open MIMIC-IV and eICU samples are too small to model. Use them to write and test your Sepsis-3 pipeline while credentialing is pending.

## Known, not confirmed

- **Paper citations:** the literature APIs returned almost nothing, so I'm citing no papers. Check the Challenge 2019 paper (Reyna et al., *Critical Care Medicine*), which the page says is the official one.
- **Contents of MIMIC-IV, eICU and AmsterdamUMCdb:** I didn't read the variable lists today. Sizes and derivation code availability are from memory.
- **Credentialing timing:** "about 1–2 weeks" is from memory.

## Not recommended, and why

- **Hugging Face copies of the Challenge data:** unofficial mirrors. Use PhysioNet.
- **Zenodo and OpenAIRE sepsis supplements:** these have mortality, biomarker or single-study tables, with no hourly vitals or onset times.
- **MIMIC-style datasets with only discharge codes:** ICD-coded sepsis gives no onset time.

## What I could not find

- An open, multi-centre dataset with raw Sepsis-3 inputs and no application.
- Working literature search results this session.
- The old Amsterdam URL (amsterdammedicaldatascience.nl/amsterdamumcdb/) returned 404, so use the DOI link.

## Next step

1. Today: download Challenge 2019 and run a baseline against its utility score.
2. Today: start the CITI course and the PhysioNet credentialing application. That is the slow part.
3. While you wait: build your Sepsis-3 derivation on the MIMIC-IV demo.
4. After approval: train on MIMIC-IV and validate externally on eICU. Ask the HiRID authors in parallel.

## Details

**1. Challenge 2019: start here today.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/challenge-2019/
- The page states there is one hourly file per subject. It has two hospital systems (sets A and B, 20,336 and 20,000 subjects).
- Labels come from Sepsis-3, and the page says they are "shifted… ahead by six hours", so the task is already 6-hour-early prediction.
- Download needs no credentials.
- Caveats:
  - It has a fixed set of about 40 variables, from memory.
  - You can't re-derive the label.
  - The challenge's hidden test set isn't included.
- Official scoring code and a utility function that rewards early predictions are provided.

**2–3. MIMIC-IV and eICU: the main research data.**
- Links: https://physionet.org/content/mimiciv/ and https://physionet.org/content/eicu-crd/
- Neither has an onset column. You derive Sepsis-3 from SOFA plus suspected infection (antibiotics and cultures). Community derivation code exists, from memory.
- Access steps, confirmed on the pages:
  1. Create a PhysioNet account.
  2. Complete CITI's "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. PhysioNet says to affiliate with "Massachusetts Institute of Technology Affiliates" and not register as an independent learner, which avoids extra fees.
  3. Upload the CITI training report, not the certificate.
  4. Apply for credentialing. A reference may be requested, from memory.
  5. Sign the data use agreement.
- Access is personal, so your supervisor and lab mates each apply separately.
- Don't send this data to hosted LLM services. PhysioNet's rule is at https://physionet.org/news/post/llm-responsible-use/.
- MIMIC-IV is single-centre. eICU is multi-centre and is the natural external-validation set.

**4. HiRID.**
- Link: https://physionet.org/content/hirid/

## Download now, no application

**PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2019, "Early Prediction of Sepsis from Clinical Data"**
- It has about 40k ICU patients from two hospital systems (training sets A and B), with hourly vitals, labs, and demographics.
- It includes a per-hour `SepsisLabel` derived from Sepsis-3 criteria. This is the only open dataset with a ready-made time-stamped onset label, so it's your week-one baseline.
- Two caveats:
  - The label is shifted about 6 hours before the Sepsis-3 onset time. That was a challenge design choice, so be careful comparing numbers with papers that don't use that shift.
  - It has only about 40 variables and no raw antibiotic or culture timestamps, so you can't re-derive onset with a different definition.
- Use the official utility score alongside AUROC and AUPRC, since prior work reports it.

The eICU demo (100 patients) is also open, but it's only useful for testing your pipeline.

## Needs credentialing (one application, several datasets)

PhysioNet credentialing is a one-time step. After it, each dataset only needs a data use agreement (DUA) signed on its page.

1. Create a PhysioNet account.
2. Complete the CITI "Data or Specimens Only Research" course, which takes a few hours.
3. Submit the credentialing form with a reference. Your supervisor is a natural choice, and they'll get an email to confirm.
4. Wait for approval. In my experience that's days to a couple of weeks, and your reference's response speed is the usual bottleneck. Start this today.
5. Sign the DUA for each dataset.

Datasets this unlocks:
- **MIMIC-IV** (single center, BIDMC) is the standard choice. The MIT-LCP `mimic-code` repo has a derived `sepsis3` table (suspected infection from antibiotics plus cultures, with SOFA ≥ 2), so you get Sepsis-3 onset times without writing the logic yourself.
- **MIMIC-III** is older. It's mostly useful for comparing against earlier papers.
- **eICU-CRD** is multi-center US (200+ hospitals), which is good for external validation. Its culture and antibiotic timing is much weaker, so Sepsis-3 onset derivation is noisier.
- **HiRID** (Bern) has high-resolution data at about 2-minute intervals. It has no official sepsis label, so you'd derive onset yourself.
- **SICdb** (Salzburg) is also on PhysioNet, I believe under the same credentialing. Check its page.

## Separate application

**AmsterdamUMCdb** is run by Amsterdam UMC. It involves a request form and a signed data sharing agreement with the institution, and it takes longer than PhysioNet. I'd treat it as a later-stage external validation set, not a week-one option.

## Tools that save time once you have access

- **ricu** (R package) harmonizes MIMIC, eICU, HiRID, and AmsterdamUMCdb into common concepts and includes a Sepsis-3 implementation.
- **YAIB** (Yet Another ICU Benchmark) has a sepsis prediction task across those datasets with preprocessing and baselines.

Both give you a consistent cohort and label definition across datasets, which matters because onset definitions differ between papers.

## Suggested plan for the week

1. **Today:** Download the Challenge data and start CITI and credentialing.
2. **Days 1–3:** Build the baseline on the Challenge data, for example gradient-boosted trees on lagged and windowed features, then a sequence model.
3. **When approved:** Sign the MIMIC-IV DUA, derive Sepsis-3 onset with `mimic-code`, and rerun your pipeline. Use eICU later for external validation.

## One compliance note

PhysioNet's DUAs generally prohibit sending the data to third-party services, including hosted LLM APIs, unless the service is covered by an approved arrangement. Check this before using any AI assistant on credentialed data, including me.

- **MIMIC-III** is older. It's mostly useful for comparing against earlier papers.
- **eICU-CRD** is multi-center US (200+ hospitals), which is good for external validation. Its culture and antibiotic timing is much weaker, so Sepsis-3 onset derivation is noisier.
- **HiRID** (Bern) has high-resolution data at about 2-minute intervals. It has no official sepsis label, so you'd derive onset yourself.
- **SICdb** (Salzburg) is also on PhysioNet, I believe under the same credentialing. Check its page.

## Separate application

**AmsterdamUMCdb** is run by Amsterdam UMC. It involves a request form and a signed data sharing agreement with the institution, and it takes longer than PhysioNet. I'd treat it as a later-stage external validation set, not a week-one option.

## Tools that save time once you have access

- **ricu** (R package) harmonizes MIMIC, eICU, HiRID, and AmsterdamUMCdb into common concepts and includes a Sepsis-3 implementation.
- **YAIB** (Yet Another ICU Benchmark) has a sepsis prediction task across those datasets with preprocessing and baselines.

Both give you a consistent cohort and label definition across datasets, which matters because onset definitions differ between papers.

## Suggested plan for the week

1. **Today:** Download the Challenge data and start CITI and credentialing.
2. **Days 1–3:** Build the baseline on the Challenge data, for example gradient-boosted trees on lagged and windowed features, then a sequence model.
3. **When approved:** Sign the MIMIC-IV DUA, derive Sepsis-3 onset with `mimic-code`, and rerun your pipeline. Use eICU later for external validation.

## One compliance note

PhysioNet's DUAs generally prohibit sending the data to third-party services, including hosted LLM APIs, unless the service is covered by an approved arrangement. Check this before using any AI assistant on credentialed data, including me.

The short version is that you can start on the PhysioNet/CinC 2019 Challenge data today, and the other useful datasets need one credentialing step. I'm answering from memory and didn't check live pages, so confirm current versions and processing times on each dataset's page.

## Download now, no application

**PhysioNet/Computing in Cardiology Challenge 2019, "Early Prediction of Sepsis from Clinical Data"**
- It has about 40k ICU patients from two hospital systems (training sets A and B), with hourly vitals, labs, and demographics.
- It includes a per-hour `SepsisLabel` derived from Sepsis-3 criteria. This is the only open dataset with a ready-made time-stamped onset label, so it's your week-one baseline.
- Two caveats:
  - The label is shifted about 6 hours before the Sepsis-3 onset time. That was a challenge design choice, so be careful comparing numbers with papers that don't use that shift.
  - It has only about 40 variables and no raw antibiotic or culture timestamps, so you can't re-derive onset with a different definition.
- Use the official utility score alongside AUROC and AUPRC, since prior work reports it.

The eICU demo (100 patients) is also open, but it's only useful for testing your pipeline.

## Needs credentialing (one application, several datasets)

PhysioNet credentialing is a one-time step. After it, each dataset only needs a data use agreement (DUA) signed on its page.

1. Create a PhysioNet account.
2. Complete the CITI "Data or Specimens Only Research" course, which takes a few hours.
3. Submit the credentialing form with a reference. Your supervisor is a natural choice, and they'll get an email to confirm.
4. Wait for approval. In my experience that's days to a couple of weeks, and your reference's response speed is the usual bottleneck. Start this today.
5. Sign the DUA for each dataset.

Datasets this unlocks:
- **MIMIC-IV** (single center, BIDMC) is the standard choice. The MIT-LCP `mimic-code` repo has a derived `sepsis3` table (suspected infection from antibiotics plus cultures, with SOFA ≥ 2), so you get Sepsis-3 onset times without writing the logic yourself.

- **eICU-CRD** is multi-center US (200+ hospitals), which is good for external validation. Its culture and antibiotic timing is much weaker, so Sepsis-3 onset derivation is noisier.
- **HiRID** (Bern) has high-resolution data at about 2-minute intervals. It has no official sepsis label, so you'd derive onset yourself.
- **SICdb** (Salzburg) is also on PhysioNet, I believe under the same credentialing. Check its page.

Both give you a consistent cohort and label definition across datasets, which matters because onset definitions differ between papers.

## Short answer
Start on the **PhysioNet/CinC 2019 Challenge data** today, and file your **PhysioNet credentialing** application at the same time. That one application unlocks MIMIC-IV, eICU, HiRID and SICdb. This is from memory, so check current version numbers and processing times on the PhysioNet pages.

## Download now, no application
**PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2019** ("Early Prediction of Sepsis from Clinical Data")
- It has about 40k ICU stays from two US hospital systems, with hourly vitals and labs (about 40 variables).
- It ships with a time-stamped `SepsisLabel` derived from Sepsis-3, so you don't have to build the label yourself.
- It's open access. It also comes with a baseline, a published utility metric and a large set of public entries to compare against.
- Caveats:
  - The label is shifted about 6 hours before the derived onset.
  - The variable set is fixed.
  - The data is a few years old.
  - A third hospital system's test set was never released.
- It's a good fit for a week-one baseline. It's too thin for a full thesis.

## Needs PhysioNet credentialing (one application, several datasets)
| Dataset | Notes for onset labels |
|---|---|
| **MIMIC-IV** (Boston, single center) | This is the main one. The `mimic-code` repo has a `sepsis3` derived concept, with suspected infection from antibiotic and culture timing plus SOFA. |
| **eICU-CRD** (US, multicenter, about 200 hospitals) | You derive onset yourself. Antibiotic and culture timing is patchier, so the labels are noisier. It's valuable for external validation. |
| **HiRID** (Bern, high-resolution) | You derive onset yourself. The Moor et al. sepsis work on HiRID and MIMIC has public label code you can reuse. |
| **SICdb** (Salzburg) | It's newer and has less published sepsis tooling. You'd derive onset yourself. |

**What the application involves:**
1. Create a PhysioNet account with your institutional email.
2. Complete the CITI "Data or Specimens Only Research" course. It takes a few hours, and you upload the completion report.
3. Fill in the credentialing form. As a student you'll need to name a reference, which is usually your supervisor, who gets an email to confirm.
4. Once you're approved, sign the data use agreement for each dataset. This is quick.

Approval often takes a few days but can stretch to a couple of weeks, so file it today.

## Needs a separate, heavier application
**AmsterdamUMCdb** (Netherlands) is granted by the Amsterdam UMC data team and ESICM. It requires a data use agreement and a project description, and it usually takes weeks. It's good for later external validation, but not for your one-week deadline.

## Practical suggestions
- **Use YAIB (Yet Another ICU Benchmark).** It harmonizes MIMIC, eICU, HiRID and AmsterdamUMCdb and includes a sepsis prediction task. That saves a lot of preprocessing and makes cross-dataset comparison easier. Check what sepsis definition it uses before relying on it.
- **Prefer MIMIC-IV over MIMIC-III.** It's newer and larger, and the `mimic-code` tooling targets it.
- **Fix your label definition early.** Sepsis-3 onset labels differ in the infection-window and SOFA-change conventions. Pick one and document it so your results are comparable across datasets.
- **Watch the data use terms.** PhysioNet's credentialed-data agreement restricts sending the data to third-party services, which includes online LLM APIs. If you plan to use AI tools on the data, check their responsible-use policy first.

## Suggested plan for the week
1. **Day 0:** Register, start CITI, and have your supervisor ready for the reference email.
2. **Day 0–1:** Download CinC 2019 and train a gradient-boosted model on hourly features with the challenge utility metric.
3. **When approved:** Move to MIMIC-IV with `sepsis3` labels, then validate on eICU or HiRID.

I can sketch the CinC 2019 baseline pipeline or the MIMIC-IV onset query if you'd like.

## Suggested plan for the week
1. **Day 0:** Register, start CITI, and have your supervisor ready for the reference email.
2. **Day 0–1:** Download CinC 2019 and train a gradient-boosted model on hourly features with the challenge utility metric.
3. **When approved:** Move to MIMIC-IV with `sepsis3` labels, then validate on eICU or HiRID.

I can sketch the CinC 2019 baseline pipeline or the MIMIC-IV onset query if you'd like.
